# Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning

Shinichi Uemura    Taiji Suzuki Affiliation: Center for Advanced
Intelligence Project, RIKEN, Tokyo, Japan Affiliation: Department of
Mathematics Informatics, The University of Tokyo
Affiliation: uemura-shinichi666@g.ecc.u-tokyo.ac.jp
Affiliation: taiji@mist.i.u-tokyo.ac.jp

###### Abstract

On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) of large language models (LLMs) has
demonstrated the ability to improve reasoning capabilities while
preserving previously acquired knowledge. Despite substantial empirical
success, the dynamics of OPSD in continual reasoning remain incompletely
understood. Modeling LLM reasoning as search over a directed acyclic
graph, we provide a unified theoretical analysis of both the dynamics of
post-training—OPSD and supervised fine-tuning (SFT) in continual
learning—and the impact of pre-training on subsequent performance. Our
findings establish three key insights with an optimization guarantee:
(i) OPSD with hints from correct outputs enables continual learning
without forgetting by sparse yet effective gradient descent updates
induced by the hint structure. (ii) SFT on correct reasoning paths can
lead to catastrophic forgetting due to dense updates along the training
paths, which overwrite the information previously acquired. (iii)
Diversity in pre-training is crucial for enabling a post-trained model
to reach a correct output when a rollout starts from an intermediate
state. Our results, supported by theoretical analysis, show that
reliable continual reasoning depends on how post-training updates
interact with the reasoning structure established during pre-training.

## 1 Introduction

Post-training has substantially improved the task-solving capabilities
of large language models through supervised fine-tuning ([Chung et al.,
2022](#bib.bib9); [Luo et al., 2025](#bib.bib4); [Zhang et al.,
2024](#bib.bib5)) and reinforcement fine-tuning ([Guo et al.,
2025](#bib.bib8); [Jin et al., 2025](#bib.bib7); [Zeng et al.,
2025](#bib.bib6)). A central distinction between common implementations
lies in the source of training trajectories: off-policy SFT learns from
fixed demonstrations ([Zhao et al., 2023](#bib.bib37); [Zhang et al.,
2023](#bib.bib38)), whereas on-policy reinforcement learning (RL) uses
reward feedback on trajectories sampled from the current model ([Wang et
al., 2023d](#bib.bib39); [Zhao et al., 2026a](#bib.bib29)). In the
latter, the generation distribution determines which experiences are
encountered during training, while itself evolving through subsequent
updates ([Kaufmann et al., 2025](#bib.bib40); [Li et al.,
2026](#bib.bib41)). The distribution inherited from pre-training
therefore influences which reasoning paths are sampled: even models with
the same initial task accuracy can distribute probability differently
across paths and risk missing some useful reasoning steps. Using a
Markovian model of reasoning, [Bu et al. (2025)](#bib.bib26) showed that
RL fine-tuning can favor high-probability paths while suppressing rare
but useful ones. For SFT, preserving existing capabilities is a central
challenge: sequential fine-tuning can cause catastrophic forgetting of
earlier tasks and degrade general capabilities ([Wang et al.,
2023b](#bib.bib42); [Li et al., 2024](#bib.bib43)). Efforts to mitigate
this problem include learning successive tasks in orthogonal low-rank
subspaces ([Wang et al., 2023a](#bib.bib44)) and applying
sharpness-aware minimization to favor flatter loss landscapes ([Li et
al., 2024](#bib.bib43)). Furthermore, in code instruction tuning,
[Biderman et al. (2024)](#bib.bib45) reported both reduced forgetting
and better preservation of output diversity with LoRA than with full
fine-tuning.

Self-distillation (SD) has also been explored as a means of learning new
tasks while preserving previously acquired capabilities. In recent
on-policy variants, the student generates a trajectory, and the same
model—conditioned on an expert demonstration or informative feedback
unavailable to the deployed student—acts as a teacher. By deriving
supervision from the model’s own behavior, self-distillation can reduce
forgetting during adaptation ([Yang et al., 2024](#bib.bib13); [Shenfeld
et al., 2026a](#bib.bib19)). They also provide token-level supervision
on student rollouts, with reported improvements in reasoning accuracy
over off-policy SFT and training efficiency over GRPO ([Hübotter et al.,
2026](#bib.bib20); [Zhao et al., 2026b](#bib.bib21)). These methods
leverage the pre-trained model’s in-context learning abilities
([Shenfeld et al., 2026a](#bib.bib19); [Hübotter et al.,
2026](#bib.bib20)), although SD can underperform RL in some settings
([Yang et al., 2026](#bib.bib1); [Kim et al., 2026](#bib.bib12)). Their
reliance on student trajectories also makes the diversity of the
pre-trained policy relevant to learning.

Despite empirical successes, the dynamics of SD and their dependence on
the pre-trained policy remain only partially understood. [Wang et al.
(2026a)](#bib.bib3), the general analysis of self-improvement,
established high-probability guarantees for true-risk improvement and
finite-sample generalization under self-modification. Existing analyses
for self-distillation characterize specific aspects of post-training.
Using a Gaussian mixture model, [Balasubramanian and Kasiviswanathan
(2026)](#bib.bib11) showed that SFT on new-task data alone drives the
mixture weight associated with previously learned behavior to zero. For
autoregressive models, [Sang et al. (2026)](#bib.bib10) bounded policy
drift within each fixed-teacher window in terms of the distillation loss
and the shift induced by a conciseness instruction. However, these
analyses directly assume the teacher distribution rather than derive it
from hint information. They consequently leave unresolved how
hints—unique to self-distillation—shape learning dynamics, which is more
difficult because hint-based analysis requires observations of the
teacher behavior and its effect on the evolving student policy through
multi-step reasoning.

Our objective is, therefore, to explain the difference in forgetting
between on-policy SD and off-policy SFT, while considering their ability
to learn new tasks. We model Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning as a
problem of reaching a task-specific goal in a DAG. This model links the
sparsity of hint information to localized updates and retention, and
provides a quantitative comparison with SFT. We evaluate this comparison
numerically in
Section [4](#S4 "4 Numerical experiments ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning").
Although existing studies suggest that pre-training is important for the
successful SD, there are few theoretical analyses on its role in
on-policy SD. Hence, as a complementary theoretical discussion, we
examine in
Section [3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 A theoretical implication for pre-training diversity ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
how limited diversity in the pre-training can leave relevant transitions
unlearned and thereby impair reasoning from intermediate states.

#### Summary of contributions

Our contributions are summarized as follows:

- •
  We model the continual learning scenario in
  Section [2](#S2 "2 Problem Setup ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
  with a graph-based approach, which is often used to explain LLM
  reasoning process such as CoT reasoning ([Abbe et al.
  (2024)](#bib.bib14); [Yang et al. (2025)](#bib.bib15); [Kim et al.
  (2025)](#bib.bib25)). In particular, we introduce a path-conflict
  setting to analyze the interference between tasks.
- •
  We prove the learnability of a single task under both SD and SFT in
  Section [3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Single-task learnability ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
  (Theorems [1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1 (Single-task learning under SD). ‣ SD as on-policy training ‣ 3.1 Single-task learnability ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
  and [2](#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2 (Single-task learning under SFT). ‣ SFT as off-policy learning ‣ 3.1 Single-task learnability ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning"),
  respectively). Notably,
  Lemma [4](#Thmlemma4 "Lemma 4 (Gradient of the SD loss). ‣ B.2 Gradient of the SD loss ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
  in
  Appendix [B.2](#A2.SS2 "B.2 Gradient of the SD loss ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
  shows that SD with a hint in
  Equation [9](#S3.E9 "In 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
  can realize a sparse update that preserves the performance of the
  pre-trained model. Furthermore, we show that SD can learn new tasks
  while preserving previously acquired capabilities, whereas SFT can
  forget them in
  Theorem [3](#Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 3 (No forgetting under SD). ‣ Sustainability of SD ‣ 3.2 Continual learning and catastrophic forgetting ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
  and
  Theorem [4](#Thmtheorem4 "Theorem 4 (Catastrophic forgetting under SFT). ‣ Forgetting in SFT ‣ 3.2 Continual learning and catastrophic forgetting ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning").
  These results are aligned with the empirical findings of [Shenfeld et
  al. (2026a)](#bib.bib19).
- •
  We conduct numerical experiments in
  Section [4](#S4 "4 Numerical experiments ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
  to support our theoretical bounds. The results confirm that tracing
  the hint-based sparse updates of SD and the overwriting properties of
  SFT is helpful to explain the observed learning dynamics.
- •
  As a complementary theoretical analysis, we study the effect of
  pre-training diversity on reasoning from intermediate states in
  Section [3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 A theoretical implication for pre-training diversity ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
  (Proposition [1](#Thmproposition1 "Proposition 1 (Restart failure beyond a rare entrance). ‣ 3.3 A theoretical implication for pre-training diversity ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")).
  We show that limited coverage in the pre-trained policy can leave
  relevant transitions unlearned even after SD succeeds from the
  original start.

These results explain how the sparsity of hint information translates
into localized updates and retention in continual reasoning. The
complementary pre-training analysis gives a theoretical implication for
reasoning from intermediate states.

### 1.1 Related work

In the following, we briefly discuss related work on self-distillation
and diversity in LLM reasoning. Additional related work is discussed in
Appendix [A](#A1 "Appendix A Additional related work ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning").

#### Self-distillation for LLMs.

Recent methods differ in the information supplied to the self-teacher.
[Shenfeld et al. (2026a)](#bib.bib19) introduced SDFT, conditioning the
teacher on pre-existing expert demonstrations to supervise
student-generated trajectories. Their experiments demonstrated new-task
acquisition with reduced forgetting relative to SFT. [Hübotter et al.
(2026)](#bib.bib20) instead conditioned the SDPO teacher on feedback
obtained after evaluating the student’s own attempts, such as execution
errors, improving reasoning accuracy and training efficiency. [Zhao et
al. (2026b)](#bib.bib21) proposed OPSD, providing the teacher with
reference answers or verified reasoning traces, and demonstrated
improved mathematical reasoning with greater token efficiency than GRPO.
[Ye et al. (2026)](#bib.bib34) introduced OPCD, including a
self-distillation setting, using knowledge extracted from past solution
traces or optimized system prompts as teacher context. Despite
substantial empirical evidence, the learning dynamics of SD remain
theoretically underexplored.

#### Diversity in LLM reasoning.

[Wang et al. (2023c)](#bib.bib22) demonstrated that aggregating answers
across diverse reasoning paths improves reasoning accuracy with
numerical experiments. [Yao et al. (2025)](#bib.bib2) proposed
diversity-aware policy optimization, which promotes token-level
diversity selectively on correct responses. Their experiments showed
improvements in both solution diversity and performance on mathematical
reasoning benchmarks. Using a Markovian model, [Bu et al.
(2025)](#bib.bib26) theoretically showed that post-training can suppress
rare but useful reasoning paths and emphasize the role of exploration in
preserving rare but useful reasoning paths already present in the
pre-trained model.

Figure 1: Example of the CoT graph we considered in
Section [2](#S2 "2 Problem Setup ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning").
Each node represents a reasoning state, and edges correspond to
reasoning transitions. Black edges indicate easy intra-cluster
transitions, while red and green ones mean difficult inter-cluster
transitions. The goal of training is to improve difficult transitions
and to reach the correct terminal. Therefore, the model is required to
learn the inter-cluster transitions while preserving the diversity
within clusters.

## 2 Problem Setup

### 2.1 Continual learning on CoT graph

Following the reasoning-graph construction of [Kim et al.
(2025)](#bib.bib25), we model the LLM chain-of-thought process as Markov
transitions on a directed acyclic graph (DAG). Let the LLM CoT graph be
$`G=(V,E)`$, where $`V`$ is the node set and $`E`$ is the directed edge
set. A model receives input $`x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ and generates a CoT
sequence $`y=(y_{0},\ldots,y_{t},\ldots,y_{T})`$, where $`y_{t}\in V`$,
$`(y_{t},y_{t+1})\in E`$, and $`T`$ denotes a rollout-dependent
termination time. Note that $`y`$ is node-simple since the graph is
acyclic. We consider a correspondence between these states and the graph
nodes; that is, each node in the graph is regarded as a reasoning state
$`y_{t}`$ like a logical sentence or a mathematical expression rather
than a simple token. Thus, the prediction goal is to reach a proper node
or cluster through logical reasoning transitions. The graph $`G`$
consists of $`K`$ clusters denoted by $`C_{1}\ldots C_{K}`$, and it is
free from multiple edges. A cluster is a set of similar reasoning states
like equivalent reformulations obtained by straightforward manipulation.
Hence, we consider the inter-cluster edges represent the essential and
conceptual transitions, while intra-cluster edges are something trivial.
For every node $`u\in C_{k}`$ and every cluster $`C_{k^{\prime}}`$ with
$`k^{\prime}\neq k`$, there is at most one node $`v\in C_{k^{\prime}}`$
such that $`(u,v)\in E`$. We separate the cluster nodes in $`C_{k}`$
into two types: one is the interior nodes denoted by
$`\mathring{C}_{k}`$, and the other is the boundary nodes denoted by
$`\partial C_{k}`$

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
\mathring{C}_{k}\mathrel{:=}\{u\in C_{k}:\forall v\in V,(u,v)\in E\Rightarrow v\in C_{k}\},\quad\partial C_{k}\mathrel{:=}C_{k}\backslash\mathring{C}_{k}.
``` |  | (1) |

For preserving the inner-cluster transition diversity, we assume that
all destinations of inter-cluster edges are interior nodes.

#### Continual learning.

We study continual learning of $`L`$ tasks
$`\mathcal{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{L}`$. The task
$`\mathcal{D}_{\ell}`$ consists of a fixed input
$`x_{\ell}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{d}`$, a fixed CoT startpoint
$`y_{\ell,0}\in\mathring{C}_{k_{\ell}}`$, and a task-specific correct
terminal cluster
$`\tilde{C}_{\ell}\in\left\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{K}\right\}`$. We assume that
$`\tilde{C}_{\ell}`$ has no outgoing inter-cluster transitions.
Furthermore, we assume that the tasks can be separated. More precisely,
the matrix $`(x_{1},\ldots,x_{L})`$ is column full rank and the clusters
$`\tilde{C}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{C}_{L}`$ are disjoint. A rollout for task
$`\mathcal{D}_{\ell}`$ is defined to be correct if and only if
$`y_{T}\in\tilde{C}_{\ell}`$. ¹¹ 1 We focus on reaching the terminal
cluster. For the subsequent search for a particular target node within
it, [Kim et al. (2025, Lemma B.9)](#bib.bib25) provide hitting-time
bounds under additional assumptions on within-cluster mixing and
stationary probabilities. Then, we consider the following assumption.

###### Assumption 1 (transition uniqueness and competition between tasks).

Each boundary node has at most one correct cluster transition for each
task. Whenever such a transition exists for multiple tasks at the same
boundary node, it is identical across any tasks that are not orthogonal
in the input. Hence, competition between tasks can arise only through
trivial interior transitions.

This assumption is strong, but seems reasonable since similar tasks
share underlying reasoning properties, which we model as common
inter-cluster transitions. $`\mathcal{P}_{\ell}`$ is the set of correct
paths for task $`\mathcal{D}_{\ell}`$, and $`E_{\ell}`$ is the union of
the inter-cluster transitions appearing in those correct paths. In
learning, the model is trained sequentially on the tasks
$`\mathcal{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{L}`$.

### 2.2 Pre-training and fine-tuning

#### Pre-training requirements

For each task $`\mathcal{D}_{\ell}`$ and cluster $`C_{k}`$, define the
set of boundary nodes that admit a correct inter-cluster transition by

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\partial^{\star}_{\ell}C_{k}=\left\{u\in\partial C_{k}\mid\exists v\in V\backslash C_{k},(u,v)\in E_{\ell}\right\}.`$ |  | (2) |

Then, we further define
$`M=\max_{k,\ell}\left|\partial^{\star}_{\ell}C_{k}\right|`$. We call
the initial node or an entry through a valid inter-cluster transition a
relevant start $`s`$ in $`C_{k}`$. For a rollout initialized at
$`Y_{0}=s`$ under task input $`x_{\ell}`$, define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\tau_{\ell,k}^{s}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:Y_{t}\in\partial C_{k}\},\quad U_{\ell,k}^{s}:=Y_{\tau_{\ell,k}^{s}}.`$ |  |

Thus $`U_{\ell,k}^{s}`$ is the first boundary node reached after the
rollout starts from the relevant start $`s`$. For the model, we consider
a softmax predictor:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbf{W}}(\cdot\mid u,x)=\text{softmax}(x^{\top}\mathbf{W}e_{u,\cdot}),\quad\mathbf{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times\left|E\right|},`$ |  | (3) |

where $`e_{u,\cdot}`$ is the embedded concatenation matrix of one-hot
vectors $`e_{u,v}`$ for all $`v\text{ s.t. }(u,v)\in E`$. The following
assumption specifies the required properties of the pre-trained
parameter $`\mathbf{W}^{(0)}`$.

###### Assumption 2 (Pre-trained diversity).

For every task $`\mathcal{D}_{\ell}`$ and non-terminal cluster $`C_{k}`$
satisfying $`\partial^{\star}_{\ell}C_{k}\neq\emptyset`$, the first
boundary-hitting time after every relevant start $`s`$ in $`C_{k}`$ is
almost surely finite. Uniformly over all such $`s`$, the pretrained
model satisfies

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{W^{(0)}}\left(U_{\ell,k}^{s}\notin\partial^{\star}_{\ell}C_{k}\mid Y_{0}=s,x_{\ell}\right)`$ | $`\displaystyle=O(\epsilon_{1}),`$ |  | (4) |
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{W^{(0)}}\left(U_{\ell,k}^{s}=u\mid Y_{0}=s,x_{\ell}\right)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\Omega(1/M),`$ |  | (5) |

for every $`u\in\partial^{\star}_{\ell}C_{k}`$. Moreover,
$`\pi_{W^{(0)}}(v\mid u,x_{\ell})=\Theta(\epsilon_{2})`$ for every
inter-cluster edge $`(u,v)`$ and every task input $`x_{\ell}`$.
$`\epsilon_{1}`$ and $`\epsilon_{2}`$ are supposed to be sufficiently
small; at least, they satisfy $`KM\epsilon_{1}=o(1)`$ and
$`M\epsilon_{2}=o(1)`$.

The diversity conditions state that (i) the pre-trained model reaches
valid boundaries with high probability, and (ii) every inter-cluster
transition initially has small but nonzero probability. In other words,
these assumptions suggest that the trivial reasoning steps are
sufficiently acquired, but essential transitions are difficult and the
model is required further training to learn.
Appendix [B.1](#A2.SS1 "B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
constructs a finite pre-trained parameter satisfying every condition of
Assumption [2](#Thmassumption2 "Assumption 2 (Pre-trained diversity). ‣ Pre-training requirements ‣ 2.2 Pre-training and fine-tuning ‣ 2 Problem Setup ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
on a family of layered DAGs.

#### Fine-tuning settings

We use SD and SFT as on- and off-policy fine-tuning methods
respectively. The detailed algorithms are described in
Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning").
For each task $`\mathcal{D}_{\ell}`$, a teacher-forcing path
$`y^{*}=(y_{0}^{*},\ldots,y_{T}^{*})`$ and a hint $`c`$ are constructed
by sampling

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\gamma\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\mathcal{P}_{\ell}).`$ |  | (6) |

Across training iterations and across elements within each mini-batch,
the teacher paths are sampled independently according to
Equation [6](#S2.E6 "In Fine-tuning settings ‣ 2.2 Pre-training and fine-tuning ‣ 2 Problem Setup ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning"),
conditional on the preceding training history. Note that for SD,
conditional on the same history, each teacher path used to construct the
hint and its corresponding unhinted student rollout are sampled
independently. We define the teacher path to be $`y^{*}=\gamma`$, and
the hint $`c`$ represents all cluster transitions in $`\gamma`$; that
is, the hint is $`c=\left\{c_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{I}`$, where
$`c_{i}=\left\{(u_{i},v_{i})\text{ is traversed.}\right\}`$. This hint
construction reflects the human instruction that ignores trivial
transitions and emphasizes the essential reasoning steps. At each
$`\mathcal{D}_{\ell}`$ training iteration, the model receives a
minibatch of $`\{x_{\ell},c^{(b)}\}_{b=1}^{B}`$ or
$`\{x_{\ell},y^{*(b)}\}_{b=1}^{B}`$ for SD or SFT respectively, and
updates the parameter $`\mathbf{W}`$ by minimizing the loss function.
Note that such an external hint is consistent with [Shenfeld et al.
(2026a)](#bib.bib19) and [Liu et al. (2026)](#bib.bib18). Since SD uses
only hints about specific transitions rather than the more informative
full paths used in SFT, pre-training is crucial for its success. We use
the same uniform distribution for SD and SFT to compare their
performance under a common source of supervision without favoring any
correct path. Batch size $`B`$ is fixed for all tasks and iterations.
The mini-batch loss functions for SD and SFT are defined as follows
respectively:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle L_{\text{SD}}(\mathbf{W};\mathcal{D}_{\ell})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{1}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\sum_{t=0}^{T^{(b)}-1}D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\pi_{\mathbf{W}}(\cdot\mid y_{t}^{(b)},x_{\ell})\parallel\text{SG}(\pi_{\mathbf{W}}(\cdot\mid y_{t}^{(b)},x_{\ell},c^{(b)}))\right),`$ |  | (7) |
|  | $`\displaystyle L_{\text{SFT}}(\mathbf{W};\mathcal{D}_{\ell})`$ | $`\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\sum_{t=0}^{T^{(b)}-1}\log\pi_{\mathbf{W}}(y_{t+1}^{*(b)}\mid y_{t}^{*(b)},x_{\ell}),`$ |  | (8) |

where SG denotes stop-gradient. The SD loss sums the per-state KL
divergences along each student rollout and averages these sums over the
$`B`$ mini-batch elements, without normalizing by rollout length. Both
SD and SFT update the parameters using SGD as
$`\mathbf{W}^{(n+1)}\to\mathbf{W}^{(n)}-\eta_{j}\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}L_{\mathrm{method}}(\mathbf{W}^{(n)};\mathcal{D}_{\ell}),`$
where $`\mathrm{method}\in\{\text{SD, SFT}\}`$ denotes the corresponding
training method.

## 3 Main results

If the hint $`c`$ strictly indicates that the model should follow all
cluster shift $`(u_{i},v_{i})`$ in $`\gamma`$, then the teacher support
would be strictly subset of the student one, and the KL divergence would
diverge. Avoiding this divergence, fix a smoothing parameter
$`\epsilon_{\mathrm{rel}}\in(0,1)`$ and use a relaxed hint $`c`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\chi_{\mathbf{W}}(c_{i}\mid y_{0:T},x)\propto\begin{cases}1&(\tau_{i}<T,y_{\tau_{i}+1}=v_{i}),\\
\epsilon_{\mathrm{rel}}&(\tau_{i}<T,y_{\tau_{i}+1}\neq v_{i}),\\
Z_{\mathbf{W},i}(x)&(\tau_{i}\geq T),\end{cases}`$ |  | (9) |

where
$`\tau_{i}=\inf\left\{s\in\{0,\ldots,T\}\mid y_{s}=u_{i}\right\}`$, with
the convention $`\inf\emptyset=\infty`$, and
$`Z_{\mathbf{W},i}(x)=\pi_{\mathbf{W}}(v_{i}\mid u_{i},x)+\epsilon_{\mathrm{rel}}(1-\pi_{\mathbf{W}}(v_{i}\mid u_{i},x))`$.
$`\chi_{\mathbf{W}}`$ is a likelihood factor for the relaxed hint
$`c_{i}`$, and $`Z_{\mathbf{W},i}(x)`$ is equivalent to the expected
local feedback before the transition from $`u_{i}`$ is observed. When
$`u_{i}`$ is not visited, using this expected value as feedback allows
us to leave the corresponding transition parameters unchanged, since the
current student provides no information about transition out of
$`u_{i}`$. This edge-level feedback enables the sparse activation, which
identifies where mistakes happen and adjusts to the student’s response
distribution ([Hübotter et al. (2026)](#bib.bib20)). From
([9](#S3.E9 "In 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")),
the teacher distribution is determined by Bayes’ rule:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbf{W}}(y_{0:T}\mid c,x)\propto\pi_{\mathbf{W}}(y_{0:T}\mid x)\cdot\chi_{\mathbf{W}}(c\mid y_{0:T},x).`$ |  | (10) |

The step-level teacher distribution
$`\pi_{\mathbf{W}}(y_{t+1}\mid y_{t},c,x)`$ is derived in
Appendix [B.2](#A2.SS2 "B.2 Gradient of the SD loss ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning").

#### Sparse structure learning.

We believe that hint-induced sparse learning mitigates catastrophic
forgetting. Indeed, as shown in
Lemma [4](#Thmlemma4 "Lemma 4 (Gradient of the SD loss). ‣ B.2 Gradient of the SD loss ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
in
Appendix [B.2](#A2.SS2 "B.2 Gradient of the SD loss ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning"),
the hint in
Equation [9](#S3.E9 "In 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
yields a gradient that updates only the parameters associated with
outgoing edges from the hint-source nodes. Consequently, SD preserves
the interior transition diversity, which is crucial for continual
learning, whereas SFT updates the parameters at all source nodes along
the teacher path.

### 3.1 Single-task learnability

#### SD as on-policy training

Before analyzing the continual learning performance, we first consider
their ability to learn a single task.
Theorems [1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1 (Single-task learning under SD). ‣ SD as on-policy training ‣ 3.1 Single-task learnability ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
and [2](#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2 (Single-task learning under SFT). ‣ SFT as off-policy learning ‣ 3.1 Single-task learnability ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
suggest that SD and SFT are both effective for solving one task. It is
important to note that there is little difference between the two
methods in learning a single task.

###### Theorem 1 (Single-task learning under SD).

Fix a task $`\mathcal{D}_{\ell}`$ and let the stepsize
$`\eta_{j}=\Theta(j^{-\nu})`$ for some $`\nu\in[0,1/2)`$. Suppose
Assumptions [1](#Thmassumption1 "Assumption 1 (transition uniqueness and competition between tasks). ‣ Continual learning. ‣ 2.1 Continual learning on CoT graph ‣ 2 Problem Setup ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
and [2](#Thmassumption2 "Assumption 2 (Pre-trained diversity). ‣ Pre-training requirements ‣ 2.2 Pre-training and fine-tuning ‣ 2 Problem Setup ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
hold and $`\epsilon_{1}`$ is sufficiently small. Define

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\displaystyle\xi_{\ell,n}^{(K)}(\delta)\mathrel{:=}\left[\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{2}^{2}\left(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon_{\mathrm{rel}}}\right)\left[\frac{e^{-\Theta(K\log\frac{1}{\epsilon_{2}})}n^{1-\nu}}{M^{K}|\mathcal{P}_{\ell}|}-\sqrt{\frac{\log(KM/\delta)}{B}}n^{1/2-\nu}\right]^{+}\right]^{-1}.
``` |  | (11) |

Then, with probability at least $`1-\delta`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbf{W}^{(n)}}\left(y_{T}\in\tilde{C}_{\ell}\mid y_{\ell,0},x_{\ell}\right)\geq 1-O\!\left(KM\epsilon_{1}\right)-O\!\left(KM\xi_{\ell,n}^{(K)}(\delta)\right).`$ |  | (12) |

Theorem [1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1 (Single-task learning under SD). ‣ SD as on-policy training ‣ 3.1 Single-task learnability ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
says that SD can learn a single task with a success probability of
$`1-O(KM\epsilon_{1})`$. The error term arises from visits to invalid
boundaries, and in this setting, we reduce this error by assuming
$`\epsilon_{1}`$ is sufficiently small. The error $`O(KM\epsilon_{1})`$
reflects a trade-off between single-task accuracy and the ability to
retain previously acquired knowledge during continual learning.
Preserving the student’s pre-trained diversity also leaves some of its
initial errors intact. On the other hand, this term might be improved by
providing hints that discourage the model from proceeding in an
incorrect direction. Indeed, [Zhu et al. (2025)](#bib.bib23) show that
penalizing incorrect responses alone can improve reasoning performance,
and their gradient analysis indicates that these updates suppress
incorrect generations and redistribute probability mass toward plausible
alternatives favored by the model’s prior. During inference, [Yao et al.
(2023)](#bib.bib24) use negative evaluations of intermediate reasoning
states to prune unpromising branches in Tree of Thoughts, with ablation
results showing that removing this pruning degrades search performance.
Theorem [1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1 (Single-task learning under SD). ‣ SD as on-policy training ‣ 3.1 Single-task learnability ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
further implies that the strength of the hint controls the convergence
rate of the student success probability. In fact, decreasing
$`\epsilon_{\mathrm{rel}}`$ reduces the bound in
Equation [11](#S3.E11 "In Theorem 1 (Single-task learning under SD). ‣ SD as on-policy training ‣ 3.1 Single-task learnability ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
without increasing the number of iterations $`n`$. Thus, the theorem
effectively captures how hint strength affects self-distillation
dynamics.

#### SFT as off-policy learning

Theorem [2](#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2 (Single-task learning under SFT). ‣ SFT as off-policy learning ‣ 3.1 Single-task learnability ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
is a counterpart of
Theorem [1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1 (Single-task learning under SD). ‣ SD as on-policy training ‣ 3.1 Single-task learnability ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
for SFT. SFT also learns a single task with high probability, and
updates the parameters at all source nodes along the teacher. It is a
main difference from SD, which only focuses on the source nodes related
to the hint.

###### Theorem 2 (Single-task learning under SFT).

Fix $`\delta\in(0,1)`$. Suppose the stepsize
$`\eta_{j}=\Theta(j^{-\nu})`$ for some $`\nu\in(0,1/2)`$ and
$`\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{SFT}}^{(0)}=\mathbf{W}^{(0)}`$. Then, with
probability at least $`1-\delta`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\min_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{\ell}}\pi_{\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{SFT}}^{(n)}}(\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(u)\mid u,x_{\ell})\geq 1-\tilde{O}\!\left(|\mathcal{P}_{\ell}|\left[\frac{1}{n^{1-\nu}}+\frac{\log(e/\delta)}{Bn^{\nu}}\right]\right).`$ |  | (13) |

Here $`\mathcal{U}_{\ell}`$ is the set of nodes appearing in the correct
paths except for the terminal, and $`\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(u)`$ is the set
of their correct successors from $`u`$. $`\tilde{O}(\cdot)`$ hides
logarithmic factors in $`n`$ and $`|\mathcal{P}_{\ell}|`$.

###### Sketch of Proof.

Note that the optimal parameter $`\mathbf{W}_{\text{opt}}`$ is not
finite because a softmax predictor with a finite parameter cannot assign
probability one to any outcome. Thus, we analyze the convergence in two
steps: (1) Set parameter
$`\mathbf{W}_{R}\to\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{opt}}(R\to\infty)`$, and use it
as a reference. (2) Construct the potential function to measure the
difference between the current parameter, the reference parameter and
the optimal one. A detailed analysis is provided in
Appendix [B.5](#A2.SS5 "B.5 Single-task learning under SFT ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning").
∎

Theorem [2](#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2 (Single-task learning under SFT). ‣ SFT as off-policy learning ‣ 3.1 Single-task learnability ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
shows that SFT learns to select correct successors from states on
correct paths. Whereas SD carries over some of the pre-trained model’s
routing errors,
Theorem [2](#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2 (Single-task learning under SFT). ‣ SFT as off-policy learning ‣ 3.1 Single-task learnability ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
shows that SFT can learn to generate correct paths with probability
approaching one. This difference reflects the scope of the updates. SFT
supervises all transitions along the teacher paths, including
intra-cluster transitions, and can therefore correct the pre-trained
routing errors reflected in the residual term of the SD lower bound. In
the next section, we use this overwriting property to explain why SFT
can forget previously learned tasks.

### 3.2 Continual learning and catastrophic forgetting

#### Sustainability of SD

In this section, we study the continual learning performance of SD and
SFT. A key feature of SD in our framework is that hints localize
parameter updates to a small subset of graph nodes while SFT fails and
overwrites the parameters of the student model. Intuitively, such
localized updates may help preserve existing knowledge and mitigate
catastrophic forgetting. Relatedly, [Wang et al. (2026b)](#bib.bib16)
suggest that self-distillation can mitigate knowledge forgetting by
selectively encouraging exploration at reasoning tokens while
maintaining stable targets for factual tokens. The following theorem
supports our intuition.

###### Theorem 3 (No forgetting under SD).

Let $`\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{SD}}^{[s]}`$ denote the parameter after
sequentially training $`\mathcal{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{s}`$ by SD.
Fix $`\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}`$, and let $`n_{\ell}`$ be the number of SD
updates used for $`\mathcal{D}_{\ell}`$. Suppose
Assumptions [1](#Thmassumption1 "Assumption 1 (transition uniqueness and competition between tasks). ‣ Continual learning. ‣ 2.1 Continual learning on CoT graph ‣ 2 Problem Setup ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
and [2](#Thmassumption2 "Assumption 2 (Pre-trained diversity). ‣ Pre-training requirements ‣ 2.2 Pre-training and fine-tuning ‣ 2 Problem Setup ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
hold for all tasks, and the stepsize $`\eta_{j}=\Theta(j^{-\nu})`$ for
some $`\nu\in[0,1/2)`$. Then, after any numbers of subsequent SD updates
on $`\mathcal{D}_{\ell+1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{L}`$, with probability at
least $`1-\delta`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{SD}}^{[L]}}\left(y_{T}\in\tilde{C}_{\ell}\mid y_{\ell,0},x_{\ell}\right)\geq 1-O\!\left(KM\epsilon_{1}\right)-O\!\left(KM\xi_{\ell,n_{\ell}}^{(K)}(\delta)\right).`$ |  | (14) |

Moreover, every correct transition learned for task
$`\mathcal{D}_{\ell}`$ satisfies

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{SD}}^{[L]}}(v\mid u,x_{\ell})\geq\pi_{\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{SD}}^{[\ell]}}(v\mid u,x_{\ell}),\qquad(u,v)\in E_{\ell}.`$ |  | (15) |

Theorem [3](#Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 3 (No forgetting under SD). ‣ Sustainability of SD ‣ 3.2 Continual learning and catastrophic forgetting ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
shows that, after learning task $`\mathcal{D}_{\ell}`$, the student can
continue learning subsequent tasks through self-distillation while
retaining its ability to generate correct rollouts from $`y_{\ell,0}`$
(Equations [14](#S3.E14 "In Theorem 3 (No forgetting under SD). ‣ Sustainability of SD ‣ 3.2 Continual learning and catastrophic forgetting ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
and [15](#S3.E15 "In Theorem 3 (No forgetting under SD). ‣ Sustainability of SD ‣ 3.2 Continual learning and catastrophic forgetting ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")).
Equation [15](#S3.E15 "In Theorem 3 (No forgetting under SD). ‣ Sustainability of SD ‣ 3.2 Continual learning and catastrophic forgetting ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
also shows that the probability of correct inter-cluster transitions may
even increase by subsequent tasks when the tasks share the same correct
inter-cluster transitions because the hints associated with these
transitions are sampled as well during training on subsequent tasks.

#### Forgetting in SFT

SFT, on the other hand, is a learning system that forces the student to
follow the teacher path, which is a relatively dense graph structure.
Therefore, if any path conflict exists between tasks, the student
rollout is likely to follow new task paths and forget the old ones. We
consider this overwriting is precisely what causes catastrophic
forgetting. For the SFT comparison, fix two tasks
$`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{old}}`$ and $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{new}}`$
learned in this order. Let $`\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{new}}^{(0)}`$ be the
parameter immediately after learning $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{old}}`$, and
let $`\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{new}}^{(n)}`$ be the parameter after $`n`$
subsequent SFT updates on $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{new}}`$. Let
$`\theta\in[0,1]`$ denote the fraction of old correct paths that
conflict with the new task. Formal definitions are deferred to
Appendix [B.7](#A2.SS7 "B.7 Catastrophic forgetting under SFT ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning").

###### Theorem 4 (Catastrophic forgetting under SFT).

Fix $`\delta\in(0,1)`$ and set
$`\lambda:=\frac{\langle x_{\mathrm{old}},x_{\mathrm{new}}\rangle}{\|x_{\mathrm{new}}\|_{2}^{2}}>0`$.
Suppose $`\eta_{j}=\Theta(j^{-\nu})`$ for some $`\nu\in(0,1/2)`$. Then,
for arbitrary pre-new-task old-path masses, with probability at least
$`1-\delta`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\displaystyle\Pi(\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{new}}^{(n)})\leq\underbrace{\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{old}}(1-\theta)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{{upper bound on}}\\
\text{{surviving knowledge}}\end{subarray}}+\widetilde{O}\!\left(\theta|\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{old}}||\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{new}}|^{\lambda}\left[\frac{1+B^{-1}\log(1/\delta)}{n^{\nu}}\right]^{\lambda}\right),
``` |  | (16) |
|  |
``` math
\displaystyle\Pi(\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{new}}^{(0)})-\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{old}}(\theta)\leq\Pi(\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{new}}^{(n)}),
``` |  | (17) |

where
$`\Pi(\mathbf{W})=\pi_{\mathbf{W}}(Y_{T}\in\tilde{C}_{\mathrm{old}}\mid Y_{0}=y_{\mathrm{old},0},x_{\mathrm{old}})`$
and

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{old}}(\phi):=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{old}}\\
|\mathcal{P}|\leq\phi|\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{old}}|\end{subarray}}\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{P}}\pi_{\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{new}}^{(0)}}(Y_{0:T}=\gamma\mid Y_{0}=y_{\mathrm{old},0},x_{\mathrm{old}}).`$ |  | (18) |

$`\tilde{O}(\cdot)`$ hides logarithmic factors in $`n`$ and
$`|\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{new}}|`$.

During sequential learning,
Theorem [4](#Thmtheorem4 "Theorem 4 (Catastrophic forgetting under SFT). ‣ Forgetting in SFT ‣ 3.2 Continual learning and catastrophic forgetting ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
shows that SFT drives the probability mass of old correct paths that
conflict with the new task toward zero as training proceeds
(Equation [16](#S3.E16 "In Theorem 4 (Catastrophic forgetting under SFT). ‣ Forgetting in SFT ‣ 3.2 Continual learning and catastrophic forgetting ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")).
Despite some forgetting, SFT retains previously acquired knowledge that
does not conflict with the new knowledge
(Equation [17](#S3.E17 "In Theorem 4 (Catastrophic forgetting under SFT). ‣ Forgetting in SFT ‣ 3.2 Continual learning and catastrophic forgetting ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")).
Although our current setting excludes conflicts between tasks at
inter-cluster transitions, this result may help explain why SD could
remain less prone to forgetting than SFT in broader settings that allow
such conflicts. Whereas SFT updates transitions along teacher paths, SD
restricts its updates only to source nodes selected by hints, which may
leave more old correct paths unaffected by subsequent training. If these
paths account for most of the old task’s success probability at the
start of new task training, their preserved solution paths would
alleviate forgetting.

###### Remark 1 (Explicit representation of $`\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{old}}(\phi)`$).

Let $`\kappa_{\max}^{\mathrm{old}}`$ and
$`\kappa_{\min}^{\mathrm{old}}`$ be the largest and smallest old-path
masses normalized by their average mass, as defined in
Appendix [B.7](#A2.SS7 "B.7 Catastrophic forgetting under SFT ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning").
Then

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{old}}(\phi)`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\Pi(\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{new}}^{(0)})\min\left\{\kappa_{\max}^{\mathrm{old}}\phi,1-\kappa_{\min}^{\mathrm{old}}+\kappa_{\min}^{\mathrm{old}}\phi\right\}.`$ |  | (19) |

###### Remark 2 (Error accumulation across tasks).

For multiple later tasks, the union of their conflicted old-path sets
can increase, and the fraction of non-conflicted old correct paths can
decrease. Consequently, $`\theta_{\mathrm{total}}`$, the multi-task
counterpart of $`\theta`$, can increase as the number of tasks grows,
thereby exacerbating the forgetting effect and further decreasing the
corresponding concentration term in the success probability of the old
tasks. This suggests an asymptotic upper bound of
$`\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{old}}(1-\theta_{\mathrm{total}})`$ on the
old-task success probability.

### 3.3 A theoretical implication for pre-training diversity

The preceding results concern new-task learning and retention from each
task’s original start. As a complementary theoretical discussion, we now
ask when the learned policy can also reason successfully from
intermediate states. The role of the pre-trained model in SD ([Shenfeld
et al., 2026a](#bib.bib19); [Hübotter et al., 2026](#bib.bib20)) and the
benefits of diverse reasoning paths ([Wang et al., 2023c](#bib.bib22))
motivate this question. We examine diversity through the coverage of
reasoning states in the pre-trained policy: because SD updates depend on
visited states, limited coverage can leave some transitions unlearned
that are needed for intermediate restarts. We fix a task
$`\mathcal{D}_{\ell}`$ and run the same SD procedure from its original
start; restarts are used only for evaluation. Under the coverage
condition of
Assumption [2](#Thmassumption2 "Assumption 2 (Pre-trained diversity). ‣ Pre-training requirements ‣ 2.2 Pre-training and fine-tuning ‣ 2 Problem Setup ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning"),
every correct boundary receives training opportunities and they suffice
to support all relevant starts simultaneously
(Proposition [3](#Thmproposition3 "Proposition 3 (Uniform restartability). ‣ B.8 Pre-training and intermediate restartability ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
in
Appendix [B.8](#A2.SS8 "B.8 Pre-training and intermediate restartability ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")).
However, if the pre-trained model is not diverse enough, some relevant
starts may not pass through proper cluster transitions and thus the
model cannot generate a correct path from them.

###### Assumption 3 (Existence of a rare entrance).

For the fixed task, retain
Assumption [2](#Thmassumption2 "Assumption 2 (Pre-trained diversity). ‣ Pre-training requirements ‣ 2.2 Pre-training and fine-tuning ‣ 2 Problem Setup ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
except its coverage lower bound at one correct boundary node $`u^{*}`$.
The pretrained probability of ever visiting $`u^{*}`$ from the original
start is $`O(1/M^{\prime})`$, with $`M^{\prime}=\Omega(M)`$ and a
uniform implied constant. Its correct transition is the only incoming
edge to a nonterminal cluster $`C_{k}`$ that does not contain the
original start $`y_{\ell,0}`$.

###### Proposition 1 (Restart failure beyond a rare entrance).

Suppose
Assumptions [1](#Thmassumption1 "Assumption 1 (transition uniqueness and competition between tasks). ‣ Continual learning. ‣ 2.1 Continual learning on CoT graph ‣ 2 Problem Setup ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
and [3](#Thmassumption3 "Assumption 3 (Existence of a rare entrance). ‣ 3.3 A theoretical implication for pre-training diversity ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
hold, $`\eta_{j}=\Theta(j^{-\nu})`$ for fixed $`\nu\in[0,1/2)`$, and
$`0<\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{\mathrm{rel}},\delta<1`$. If

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\displaystyle n=\Omega\!\left(\max\left\{\frac{\log(2KM/\delta)}{B\underline{\rho}_{\ell,K,M}^{\,2}}\right.,\left.\left[\frac{\epsilon_{1}^{-1}-1}{\epsilon_{2}^{2}\log(1/\epsilon_{\mathrm{rel}})\underline{\rho}_{\ell,K,M}}\right]^{1/(1-\nu)}\right\}\right),
``` |  | (20) |
|  |
``` math
\displaystyle M^{\prime}=\Omega\!\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon_{2}^{|E_{\ell}|}}\max\!\left\{\frac{1}{KM\epsilon_{1}},\frac{Bn}{\delta}\right\}\right),
``` |  | (21) |

with sufficiently large uniform constants, then, with probability at
least $`1-\delta`$, no training rollout visits $`u^{*}`$, and jointly

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbf{W}^{(n)}}(y_{T}\in\tilde{C}_{\ell}\mid y_{\ell,0},x_{\ell})=1-O(KM\epsilon_{1})\quad(\text{{Misleading high accuracy}}),
``` |  | (22) |
|  |
``` math
\displaystyle\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}_{\ell,k}}\pi_{\mathbf{W}^{(n)}}(y_{T}\in\tilde{C}_{\ell}\mid y_{0}=s,x_{\ell})=O(M\epsilon_{2})\quad(\text{{Restart failure}}).
``` |  | (23) |

Here $`\mathcal{S}_{\ell,k}`$ contains the relevant starts in $`C_{k}`$
and $`\underline{\rho}_{\ell,K,M}`$ is specified in
Appendix [B.3](#A2.SS3 "B.3 Learning hint-supported inter-cluster transitions ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning").

Proposition [1](#Thmproposition1 "Proposition 1 (Restart failure beyond a rare entrance). ‣ 3.3 A theoretical implication for pre-training diversity ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
states that if $`C_{k}`$ is a difficult cluster for the student to reach
and the amount of training is insufficient, the student may still have a
low success probability when restarting from relevant intermediate
states in $`C_{k}`$ even though the model seems to have highly learned
the task from its original start $`y_{\ell,0}`$
(Equation [22](#S3.E22 "In Proposition 1 (Restart failure beyond a rare entrance). ‣ 3.3 A theoretical implication for pre-training diversity ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")).
Rare entrances satisfying
Assumption [3](#Thmassumption3 "Assumption 3 (Existence of a rare entrance). ‣ 3.3 A theoretical implication for pre-training diversity ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
make some clusters severely difficult to reach from the original start,
and thus leave these clusters unlearned, preventing the success
probability from increasing. To overcome this issue, self-distillation
requires an amount of training that grows at least linearly with
$`M^{\prime}`$. The gap in success probabilities between the original
start and the downstream cluster suggests that limited diversity can
pose risks at inference time that are difficult to detect during
training. Improved performance from the original start may not translate
into better performance when restarting from downstream clusters.
Pretrained coverage therefore supports intermediate restartability
beyond original-start accuracy.

###### Remark 3 (Separation of training times).

Under
Assumptions [1](#Thmassumption1 "Assumption 1 (transition uniqueness and competition between tasks). ‣ Continual learning. ‣ 2.1 Continual learning on CoT graph ‣ 2 Problem Setup ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
and [2](#Thmassumption2 "Assumption 2 (Pre-trained diversity). ‣ Pre-training requirements ‣ 2.2 Pre-training and fine-tuning ‣ 2 Problem Setup ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning"),
the model trained by SD can resume from any relevant start for a
training length satisfying
Equation [20](#S3.E20 "In Proposition 1 (Restart failure beyond a rare entrance). ‣ 3.3 A theoretical implication for pre-training diversity ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
(Proposition [3](#Thmproposition3 "Proposition 3 (Uniform restartability). ‣ B.8 Pre-training and intermediate restartability ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
in
Appendix [B.8](#A2.SS8 "B.8 Pre-training and intermediate restartability ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")).
Holding other quantities fixed, the nonvisit horizon grows linearly with
$`M^{\prime}`$. If the nonvisit horizon grows asymptotically faster than
the sufficient learning scale, there is a range of training lengths for
which the two settings exhibit different restart behavior. Under the
full-coverage condition, the trained model can reason successfully from
every relevant start, whereas under the rare-entrance condition, the
cluster beyond the rare entrance remains unlearned.

## 4 Numerical experiments

In this section, we evaluate whether the theoretical bounds are
effective. The number of tasks is set to $`L=4`$. Additional
experimental details are written in
Appendix [C](#A3 "Appendix C Numerical experiment ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning").
Figure 2 shows training curves for every task between SD and SFT in
continual learning. SFT exhibits a significant drop in performance on
task 1 to 3 while SD preserves each task’s performance during
subsequent-task training. These results confirm that tracing the sparse
updates in SD and the conflict between tasks in SFT can explain the
observed retention and forgetting

Figure 2: Training dynamics under SD (top row) and SFT (bottom row) for
Tasks 1–4. Solid curves show the mean correct-generation probability
over ten seeds, and shaded regions indicate the minimum–maximum range.
Dash-dotted and dashed black curves represent the SD population learning
references and conditional retention lower bounds. Dashed and dotted
black curves represent the SFT conditional retention lower bounds and
population upper references, respectively. Population references use
$`B\to\infty`$ for stabilizing the curves. Purple dash-dotted lines show
the concentration-profile term
$`\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{old}}(1-\theta_{\mathrm{total}})`$. Vertical
gray lines indicate task transitions.

## 5 Conclusion

We analyze continual learning under SD and SFT using a CoT reasoning
graph. Our theory shows how the sparsity of hint information localizes
SD updates to preserve previously acquired capabilities, whereas dense
SFT updates along the teacher paths can cause forgetting. As a
complementary theoretical implication, we show that limited coverage in
the pre-trained policy can prevent successful reasoning from
intermediate states even after SD succeeds from the original start.

#### Future work.

The major improvement is to extend the analysis from the current outcome
reward format such as
Equation [9](#S3.E9 "In 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
to that of the process reward. As shown in [Hübotter et al.
(2026)](#bib.bib20), the self-teacher in on-policy self-distillation can
act as an implicit process reward model through retrospection when
provided with rich feedback. This setting might also be helpful to
analyze the importance of in-context learning ability in the
pre-training strategy.

## AI use statement

We used AI, mainly ChatGPT, to assist in the following ways.

- •
  To aid and polish writing: We used AI to improve the clarity and
  grammar of the text, and to help us express our ideas more clearly.
- •
  For retrieval of relevant references and related work: Mainly, we used
  AI to find and summarize relevant works and we incorporated them into
  this paper.
- •
  For research ideation: We used AI to brainstorm ideas and to discuss
  the research direction.
  Assumption [1](#Thmassumption1 "Assumption 1 (transition uniqueness and competition between tasks). ‣ Continual learning. ‣ 2.1 Continual learning on CoT graph ‣ 2 Problem Setup ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning"),
  Equations [4](#S2.E4 "In Assumption 2 (Pre-trained diversity). ‣ Pre-training requirements ‣ 2.2 Pre-training and fine-tuning ‣ 2 Problem Setup ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
  and [5](#S2.E5 "In Assumption 2 (Pre-trained diversity). ‣ Pre-training requirements ‣ 2.2 Pre-training and fine-tuning ‣ 2 Problem Setup ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
  in
  Assumption [2](#Thmassumption2 "Assumption 2 (Pre-trained diversity). ‣ Pre-training requirements ‣ 2.2 Pre-training and fine-tuning ‣ 2 Problem Setup ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
  and
  Section [3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 A theoretical implication for pre-training diversity ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
  is constructed during the discussion with AI.
- •
  For the purpose of drafting sections of all contents in the paper: We
  made AI-generated drafts at all sections, which were then revised and
  modified repeatedly.
- •
  For numerical experiments: We made AI create some code and the graph
  we used to validate our theoretical results. These codes and graphs
  were revised and modified by us to fit our purpose.
- •
  For proving mathematical claims: AI helped us to check the correctness
  of the proofs and formalized our ideas to mathematical statements and
  provided proofs. In particular, the following is a list of the major
  works of AI in the proofs.

  - –
    Lemma [9](#Thmlemma9 "Lemma 9 (Update opportunities from retained coverage). ‣ Shared notation and constants. ‣ B.3 Learning hint-supported inter-cluster transitions ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning"):
    AI helped us to evaluate the times of visitation to the correct
    boundaries.
  - –
    Lemma [13](#Thmlemma13 "Lemma 13 (Convex potential, unbiased drift, and support error). ‣ Sketch of proof of Theorem . ‣ B.5 Single-task learning under SFT ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning"):
    AI helped us to define the potential function in
    Equation [65](#A2.E65 "In Sketch of proof of Theorem . ‣ B.5 Single-task learning under SFT ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
    and then incorporated it into our original proof to produce the
    current version.
  - –
    Theorem [4](#Thmtheorem4 "Theorem 4 (Catastrophic forgetting under SFT). ‣ Forgetting in SFT ‣ 3.2 Continual learning and catastrophic forgetting ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning"):
    AI helped us to prove the bounds in
    Theorem [4](#Thmtheorem4 "Theorem 4 (Catastrophic forgetting under SFT). ‣ Forgetting in SFT ‣ 3.2 Continual learning and catastrophic forgetting ‣ 3 Main results ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning").

## Reproducibility

The code, configuration files, and saved results for reproducing the
numerical experiments are available in the
[continual-reasoning-experiments GitHub
repository](https://github.com/yuukiizayoi/continual-reasoning-experiments).²²
2
[https://github.com/yuukiizayoi/continual-reasoning-experiments](https://github.com/yuukiizayoi/continual-reasoning-experiments)
The repository README provides instructions for rerunning training and
regenerating the figures from saved checkpoints. The code implements the
synthetic CoT graph and the SD and SFT training procedures. Experimental
settings and results are given in
Appendix [C](#A3 "Appendix C Numerical experiment ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning").

## Appendix A Additional related work

In this section, we discuss additional related work.

#### On-policy versus off-policy learning.

A central focus of this paragraph is the preservation of previously
acquired capabilities. Continual SFT can degrade existing knowledge and
reasoning abilities through catastrophic forgetting ([Luo et al.,
2023](#bib.bib30)). Recent comparisons report that RL often preserves
prior capabilities better than SFT at comparable new-task performance
([Lai et al., 2025](#bib.bib31); [Shenfeld et al., 2026b](#bib.bib28);
[Chen et al., 2025](#bib.bib32)). Controlled experiments further
identify on-policy sampling as an important contributor to this
retention advantage ([Chen et al., 2025](#bib.bib32)). [Zhu et al.
(2026)](#bib.bib33) attribute the contrasting forgetting behavior of SFT
and RL to inter-task interference: SFT exhibits substantial conflicts
during sequential training, whereas RL induces less interferences that
allow multiple task capabilities to coexist. From a complementary
distributional perspective, [Shenfeld et al. (2026b)](#bib.bib28) argue
that standard SFT fits external demonstrations without explicitly
accounting for proximity to the base model, whereas on-policy updates
are biased toward solutions closer to the initial policy, helping
explain their reduced forgetting. Building on [Zhu et al.
(2026)](#bib.bib33)’s task-interference perspective, we develop more
theoretical analysis of how SFT and on-policy SD differ in learning new
tasks and preserving previously acquired capabilities.

#### CoT reasoning as graph search.

Graph reasoning is a broadly accepted approach for analyzing the
reasoning capabilities of language models. [Sanford et al.
(2024)](#bib.bib35) characterize Transformer expressivity on graph
reasoning tasks in terms of depth, width, and additional computation
tokens, and empirically evaluate their theoretical predictions on
GraphQA. [Bachmann and Nagarajan (2024)](#bib.bib36) use path-star
graphs to demonstrate how teacher-forced next-token prediction can favor
shortcuts that hinder learning to plan. [Kim et al. (2025)](#bib.bib25)
model CoT as a metastable Markov process on clustered graphs and
establish benefits of search, RL, and distillation for crossing rare
inter-cluster transitions. [Wei and Kim (2026)](#bib.bib27) study
backtracking in graph pathfinding and prove an exponential
inference-time advantage of RLVR over SFT when SFT is trained only on
golden shortest paths without negative examples. Our work uses a CoT
graph model to analyze how SFT and self-distillation affect the
retention of what they learned.

## Appendix B Realizability and proofs

The proofs are organized as sketches, supporting lemmas, and main
proofs.
Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
describes the SD and SFT training procedures.

1: method $`\in\{\text{SD, SFT}\}`$

2: $`\mathbf{W}^{(0)}`$, $`B`$, $`\{n_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{L}`$,
$`\{\eta_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}`$, $`\epsilon_{\mathrm{rel}}\in(0,1)`$

3: $`\mathbf{W}\leftarrow\mathbf{W}^{(0)}`$

4: for $`\ell=1,\ldots,L`$ do

5:   for $`j=1,\ldots,n_{\ell}`$ do

6:    for $`b=1,\ldots,B`$ do

7:      Sample $`y^{(b)}\sim\pi_{\mathbf{W}}(\cdot\mid x_{\ell})`$ and
$`\gamma^{(b)}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\mathcal{P}_{\ell})`$.

8:      if method $`=`$ SFT then

9:       $`y^{*(b)}\leftarrow\gamma^{(b)}`$

10:      else

11:       Construct $`c^{(b)}`$ from all inter-cluster transitions in
$`\gamma^{(b)}`$.

12:       Construct $`\pi_{\mathbf{W}}(\cdot\mid x_{\ell},c^{(b)})`$.

13:      end if

14:    end for

15:
   $`\mathbf{W}\leftarrow\mathbf{W}-\eta_{j}\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}L_{\mathrm{method}}(\mathbf{W};\mathcal{D}_{\ell})`$

16:   end for

17: end for

18: return $`\mathbf{W}`$

Algorithm 1 Sequential SD and SFT in the reasoning-graph model

### B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs

We consider layered DAGs satisfying the support conditions below. For
this graph family, the proposition following the description shows that
Assumption [2](#Thmassumption2 "Assumption 2 (Pre-trained diversity). ‣ Pre-training requirements ‣ 2.2 Pre-training and fine-tuning ‣ 2 Problem Setup ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
can be realized by a finite pre-trained parameter $`\mathbf{W}^{(0)}`$.
For $`u\in V`$, write $`\mathcal{N}(u):=\{v\in V:(u,v)\in E\}`$. Suppose
that $`G`$ is a finite layered DAG, meaning that

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle V=V_{0}\mathbin{\dot{\cup}}\cdots\mathbin{\dot{\cup}}V_{H},\qquad(u,v)\in E,\ u\in V_{h},\ v\in V_{h^{\prime}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad h<h^{\prime}.`$ |  | (24) |

We call such a graph *routing-compatible* for the task family if the
following conditions hold:

1.  1.
    Every boundary node has at least one same-cluster successor:

    |  |  |  |  |
    |----|----|----|----|
    |  | $`\displaystyle R_{k}(u):=\mathcal{N}(u)\cap C_{k}\neq\emptyset,\qquad u\in\partial C_{k}.`$ |  | (25) |

    These later-layer nodes provide support for the residual probability
    mass of a finite softmax distribution; they are not thereby declared
    relevant starts.
2.  2.
    Every relevant start belongs to the interior of its cluster, and for
    every task $`\mathcal{D}_{\ell}`$, every non-terminal cluster
    $`C_{k}`$ with $`\partial_{\ell}^{\star}C_{k}\neq\emptyset`$, and
    every relevant start $`s`$ in $`C_{k}`$,

    |  |  |  |  |
    |----|----|----|----|
    |  | $`\displaystyle\partial_{\ell}^{\star}C_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{N}(s)\subseteq\partial C_{k}.`$ |  | (26) |

The numbers of layers, clusters, nodes, valid boundaries, and residual
successors are arbitrary within this graph family.

###### Proposition B.1 (Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs).

Suppose that $`G`$ is a routing-compatible layered DAG in the sense of
Equations [24](#A2.E24 "In B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")–[26](#A2.E26 "In item 2 ‣ B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning").
Under the full-rank condition on $`(x_{1},\ldots,x_{L})`$ in the problem
setup, fix $`\epsilon_{1}\in(0,1)`$ and $`\epsilon_{2}>0`$ such that

|     |                                                    |     |      |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     | $`\displaystyle(K-1)\epsilon_{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}.`$ |     | (27) |

Then there exists a finite
$`\mathbf{W}^{(0)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times|E|}`$ satisfying every clause
of
Assumption [2](#Thmassumption2 "Assumption 2 (Pre-trained diversity). ‣ Pre-training requirements ‣ 2.2 Pre-training and fine-tuning ‣ 2 Problem Setup ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
simultaneously for all task inputs $`x_{1},\ldots,x_{L}`$.

#### Sketch of proof of Proposition [B.1](#A2.Thmrealizabilityproposition1 "Proposition B.1 (Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs). ‣ B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning").

- •
  Construct positive within-cluster routing kernels with small
  invalid-boundary probability and uniform coverage of valid boundaries
  (Lemma [1](#Thmlemma1 "Lemma 1 (A positive routing kernel with boundary coverage). ‣ Sketch of proof of Proposition . ‣ B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")).
- •
  Construct normalized boundary kernels assigning the prescribed small
  probability to every inter-cluster edge
  (Lemma [2](#Thmlemma2 "Lemma 2 (Boundary kernels with small inter-cluster probabilities). ‣ Sketch of proof of Proposition . ‣ B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")).
- •
  Realize all task-wise kernels simultaneously with one finite softmax
  parameter
  (Lemma [3](#Thmlemma3 "Lemma 3 (Simultaneous finite softmax realization). ‣ Sketch of proof of Proposition . ‣ B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")).

###### Lemma 1 (A positive routing kernel with boundary coverage).

Under
Equation [26](#A2.E26 "In item 2 ‣ B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning"),
there is a strictly positive kernel at every relevant start for which
the first boundary is reached in one step, the invalid-boundary
probability is at most $`\epsilon_{1}`$, and each valid boundary has
probability at least $`1/(2M)`$.

###### Proof.

Fix a task $`\mathcal{D}_{\ell}`$, a non-terminal cluster $`C_{k}`$ with
$`\partial_{\ell}^{\star}C_{k}\neq\emptyset`$, and a relevant start
$`s`$ in $`C_{k}`$. Define

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle I_{\ell,k}(s):=\mathcal{N}(s)\setminus\partial_{\ell}^{\star}C_{k}.`$ |  | (28) |

By
Equation [26](#A2.E26 "In item 2 ‣ B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning"),
every outgoing neighbor of $`s`$ is a boundary node and every valid
boundary is an outgoing neighbor. Prescribe the transition kernel at
$`s`$ by

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle P_{\ell}(v\mid s):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+\epsilon_{1})|\partial_{\ell}^{\star}C_{k}|},&v\in\partial_{\ell}^{\star}C_{k},\quad I_{\ell,k}(s)\neq\emptyset,\\[7.0pt]
\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon_{1}}{(1+\epsilon_{1})|I_{\ell,k}(s)|},&v\in I_{\ell,k}(s),\quad I_{\ell,k}(s)\neq\emptyset,\\[7.0pt]
\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\partial_{\ell}^{\star}C_{k}|},&v\in\partial_{\ell}^{\star}C_{k},\quad I_{\ell,k}(s)=\emptyset.\end{cases}`$ |  | (29) |

This is a strictly positive probability distribution on
$`\mathcal{N}(s)`$. Because $`\mathcal{N}(s)\subseteq\partial C_{k}`$,
the first boundary is reached in exactly one step, and hence

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P_{\ell}}\!\left(U_{\ell,k}^{s}\notin\partial_{\ell}^{\star}C_{k}\mid Y_{0}=s,x_{\ell}\right)`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon_{1}}{1+\epsilon_{1}}\leq\epsilon_{1},`$ |  | (30) |
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P_{\ell}}\!\left(U_{\ell,k}^{s}=u\mid Y_{0}=s,x_{\ell}\right)`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{(1+\epsilon_{1})|\partial_{\ell}^{\star}C_{k}|}\geq\frac{1}{2M}`$ |  | (31) |

for every $`u\in\partial_{\ell}^{\star}C_{k}`$. Thus the first-boundary
hitting time is almost surely finite, the routing error is
$`O(\epsilon_{1})`$, and every valid boundary receives probability
$`\Omega(1/M)`$, uniformly over all relevant starts. ∎

###### Lemma 2 (Boundary kernels with small inter-cluster probabilities).

Under
Equation [25](#A2.E25 "In item 1 ‣ B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
and
Equation [27](#A2.E27 "In Proposition B.1 (Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs). ‣ B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning"),
each boundary admits a strictly positive normalized kernel assigning
exactly $`\epsilon_{2}`$ to every inter-cluster edge.

###### Proof.

We next prescribe the kernel at boundary nodes. For
$`u\in\partial C_{k}`$, let

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle d_{u,k}^{\mathrm{out}}:=|\mathcal{N}(u)\setminus C_{k}|.`$ |  | (32) |

For every task input, set

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle P_{\ell}(v\mid u):=\begin{cases}\epsilon_{2},&v\in\mathcal{N}(u)\setminus C_{k},\\[4.0pt]
\displaystyle\frac{1-d_{u,k}^{\mathrm{out}}\epsilon_{2}}{|R_{k}(u)|},&v\in R_{k}(u).\end{cases}`$ |  | (33) |

The graph convention in the problem setup gives
$`d_{u,k}^{\mathrm{out}}\leq K-1`$. Therefore
Equation [27](#A2.E27 "In Proposition B.1 (Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs). ‣ B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning"),
together with
Equation [25](#A2.E25 "In item 1 ‣ B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning"),
makes
Equation [33](#A2.E33 "In Proof. ‣ Sketch of proof of Proposition . ‣ B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
a strictly positive probability distribution. ∎

###### Lemma 3 (Simultaneous finite softmax realization).

Every family of strictly positive task-wise transition kernels
$`P_{\ell}`$ on the given edge support is realized by one finite
$`\mathbf{W}^{(0)}`$ when the task-input matrix has full column rank.

###### Proof.

For each task input, assign any strictly positive probability
distribution to the outgoing edges of each remaining node whose kernel
has not yet been specified. No kernel is needed at a terminal node with
no outgoing edge. We now realize all these task-wise kernels with a
single finite parameter matrix. Let

|     |                                                       |     |      |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     | $`\displaystyle X:=\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}^{\top}\\
       \vdots\\
       x_{L}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}.`$  |     | (34) |

The full-rank input condition gives $`\operatorname{rank}(X)=L`$, so
$`XX^{\top}`$ is invertible. For every $`(u,v)\in E`$, define

|  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\ell_{u,v}`$ | $`\displaystyle:={}\begin{bmatrix}\log P_{1}(v\mid u)\\
\vdots\\
\log P_{L}(v\mid u)\end{bmatrix},`$ | $`\displaystyle\mathbf{W}^{(0)}e_{u,v}`$ | $`\displaystyle:={}X^{\top}(XX^{\top})^{-1}\ell_{u,v}.`$ |  | (35) |

All prescribed edge probabilities are strictly positive, so
$`\mathbf{W}^{(0)}`$ is finite, and

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle x_{\ell}^{\top}\mathbf{W}^{(0)}e_{u,v}=\log P_{\ell}(v\mid u).`$ |  | (36) |

Since $`P_{\ell}(\cdot\mid u)`$ is normalized over $`\mathcal{N}(u)`$,
the softmax predictor satisfies

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbf{W}^{(0)}}(v\mid u,x_{\ell})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{\exp(\log P_{\ell}(v\mid u))}{\sum_{w\in\mathcal{N}(u)}\exp(\log P_{\ell}(w\mid u))}=P_{\ell}(v\mid u).`$ |  | (37) |

∎

#### Main proof of Proposition [B.1](#A2.Thmrealizabilityproposition1 "Proposition B.1 (Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs). ‣ B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning").

###### Proof.

Lemmas [1](#Thmlemma1 "Lemma 1 (A positive routing kernel with boundary coverage). ‣ Sketch of proof of Proposition . ‣ B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
and [2](#Thmlemma2 "Lemma 2 (Boundary kernels with small inter-cluster probabilities). ‣ Sketch of proof of Proposition . ‣ B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
specify compatible kernels, since relevant starts are interior nodes.
Lemma [3](#Thmlemma3 "Lemma 3 (Simultaneous finite softmax realization). ‣ Sketch of proof of Proposition . ‣ B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
realizes them simultaneously. Finally,
Equation [24](#A2.E24 "In B.1 Realizability on routing-compatible layered DAGs ‣ Appendix B Realizability and proofs ‣ Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning")
implies that every transition strictly increases the layer index.
````
